Finally, the following theorem lifts all the results from timed input/output transition systems to the symbolic representation level.
Similarly to Theorem
7
, we need to take the special case from Figure
9
into account (but now consider action
c
to be an input for
A^{2}
). The transition in Figure
9
(e) from
(1,4)\xlongrightarrow{a!}(2,4)
can be ‘removed’ with adversarial pruning by realizing that the target state
(2,4)
is an inconsistent state (you can see this by noticing that no time delay, including a zero time delay, is possible).

Theorem 9
.

Given two specification automata
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\allowbreak\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset
. Then
(\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}=(\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
.

Before we can prove this theorem, we have to introduce several lemmas. These lemmas are almost identical to the ones in Section
4
for the conjunction. Therefore, we have omitted the proof.

Lemma 11
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset
. Then
Q^{\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=Q^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
and
q_{0}^{\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=q_{0}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
.

Lemma 12
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset
. Denote
X=\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
Y=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and let
d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q^{X}\cap Q^{Y}
. Then
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
if and only if
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
.

Lemma 13
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset
. Denote
X=\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
Y=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and let
a\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}^{2}
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q^{X}\cap Q^{Y}
. Then
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
if and only if
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
.

Lemma 14
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset
. Denote
X=\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
Y=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and let
a\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{2}
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q^{X}\cap Q^{Y}
, where
q_{2}=(l_{2}^{1},l_{2}^{2},v_{2})
. If
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{2})
, then
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
if and only if
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
.

Corollary 3
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset
. Denote
X=\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
Y=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and let
a\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{2}
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q^{X}\cap Q^{Y}
. If
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
, then
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
.

Lemma 15
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset
. Let
Q\subseteq\mathit{Loc}^{1}\times\mathit{Loc}^{2}\times[(\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cup\mathit{Clk}^{2})\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
. Then
\mathrm{err}^{\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(Q)=\mathrm{err}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(Q)
.

Lemma 16
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset
. Then
\mathrm{cons}^{\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=\mathrm{cons}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
.

Finally, we are ready to proof Theorem
9
.

Proof of Theorem
9
.

We will prove this theorem by showing that
(\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
and
(\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
have the same set of states, same initial state, same set of actions, and same transition relation.

It follows from Lemma
11
that
\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
have the same state set and initial state. As
\mathrm{cons}^{\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=\mathrm{cons}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=\mathrm{cons}
from Lemma
16
, it follows that
(\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
and
(\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
have the same state set and initial state. Also, observe that the semantic of a TIOA and adversarial pruning do not alter the action set. Therefore, it follows directly that
(\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
and
(\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
have the same action set and partitioning into input and output actions.

It remains to show that
(\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
and
(\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
have the same transition relation. In the remainder of the proof, we will use
v^{1}
and
v^{2}
to indicate the part of a valuation
v
of only the clocks of
A^{1}
and
A^{2}
, respectively. Also, for brevity we write
X=(\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
,
Y=(\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
, and
\mathit{Clk}=\mathit{Clk}^{1}\uplus\mathit{Clk}^{2}
in the rest of this proof.

(
\Rightarrow
) Assume a transition
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
in
X
. From Definition
12
it follows that
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
in
\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
q_{2}^{X}\in\mathrm{cons}
. Following Definition
3
of the semantic, it follows that there exists an edge
(l_{1},a,\varphi,c,l_{2})\in E^{A^{1}\parallel A^{2}}
with
q_{1}^{X}=(l_{1},v_{1})
,
q_{2}^{X}=(l_{2},v_{2})
,
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathit{Loc}^{A^{1}\parallel A^{2}}
,
v_{1},v_{2}\in[\mathit{Clk}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
,
v_{1}\models\varphi
,
v_{2}=v_{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}
, and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}(l_{2})
. Now we consider the three cases of Definition
17
of the parallel composition for TIOA.

•

a\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}^{2}
. It follows directly from Lemma
13
that
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Since
q_{2}^{X}\in\mathrm{cons}
, it holds that
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
Y
.

•

a\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{2}
. It follows directly from Corollary
3
that
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Since
q_{2}^{X}\in\mathrm{cons}
, it holds that
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
Y
.

•

a\in\mathit{Act}^{2}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{1}
. It follows directly from Corollary
3
(where we switched
A^{1}
and
A^{2}
) that
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Since
q_{2}^{X}\in\mathrm{cons}
, it holds that
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
Y
.

Now consider that
a
is a delay
d
. It follows directly from Lemma
12
that
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{d}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Since
q_{2}^{X}\in\mathrm{cons}
, it holds that
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{d}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
Y
.

We have shown that when
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
X=(\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
, it holds that
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
Y=(\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
. Since the transition is arbitrarily chosen, it holds for all transitions in
X
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Assume a transition
q_{1}^{Y}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{Y}
in
Y
. From Definition
12
it follows that
q_{1}^{Y}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{Y}
in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
q_{2}^{Y}\in\mathrm{cons}
. Now we consider the three cases of Definition
15
of the parallel composition for TIOTS.

•

a\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}^{2}
. It follows directly from Lemma

that
q_{1}^{Y}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{Y}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Since
q_{2}^{Y}\in\mathrm{cons}
, it holds that
q_{1}^{Y}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{Y}
is a transition in
X
.

•

. From time reflexivity of Definition
1
we have that
q_{2}^{Y}\xlongrightarrow{d}
with

. From Definitions
12
and

it follows that
q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}\xlongrightarrow{d}
and

. Now, from Definition

it follows that
v^{2}+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
, i.e.,

.

It now follows directly from Lemma

q_{1}^{Y}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{Y}

. Since
q_{2}^{Y}\in\mathrm{cons}
, it holds that
q_{1}^{Y}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{Y}
is a transition in

.

•

. From time reflexivity of Definition
1
we have that
q_{2}^{Y}\xlongrightarrow{d}
with

. From Definitions
12
and
15
it follows that
q^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}\xlongrightarrow{d}
and
q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}\xlongrightarrow{d}
. Now, from Definition

v^{1}+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l^{1})
, i.e.,
v^{1}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l^{1})

It now follows directly from Lemma

(where we switched
A^{1}
and
A^{2}

We have shown that when

∎

Finally, the following corollary describes a special case of Theorem
9
, which happens to be one of the unproven main theorems in our previous work
david_real-time_2015
.

Corollary 4
.

Given two specification automata
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\allowbreak\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset
and
\mathit{Act}^{1}=\mathit{Act}^{2}
. Then
\llbracket A^{1}\parallel A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\parallel\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
.

Proof.

This corollary follows directly as a special case from the proof of Theorem
9
. The special case only depends on Lemmas
11
and
13
, which do not require adversarial pruning to be applied.

\mathit{Act}_{i}^{T}

Figure 12
:
Concept of quotient for given specifications
S
and
T
and the unknown implementation
X
.

An essential operator in a complete specification theory is the one of
quotienting
. It allows for factoring out behavior from a larger component. If one has a large component specification
T
and a small one
S
then
T\backslash\!\backslash S
is the specification of exactly those components that when composed with
S
refine
T
. (
In this context, larger does not necessarily means bigger in terms of the size of the state set, action set or transition relation, but more like higher in the refinement hierarchy.
) In other words,
T\backslash\!\backslash S
specifies the work that still needs to be done, given availability of an implementation of
S
, in order to provide an implementation of
T
.

Figure
12
shows the conceptual idea behind the quotient operator, which is formalized in Theorem
10
later in this section. Given two specification
S
and
T
, each with its own action sets, the purpose of the quotient operator is to calculate the specification for the missing implementation
X
. Once
X
is put parallel to
S
, the combined system should refine
T
. From this figure, we can derive the true minimal requirement on the quotient operator (our previous work had stricter requirements):
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{S}\cap\mathit{Act}_{i}^{T}=\emptyset
, i.e., an action cannot be classified as an output action in
S
and an input action in
T
.

We proceed like for structural and logical compositions and start with a
quotient
that may introduce error states. Those errors
can
then pruned
if desired
.

Definition 18
.

Given specifications
S=(Q^{S},q_{0}^{S},\mathit{Act}^{S},\rightarrow^{S})
and
T=(Q^{T},q_{0}^{T},\allowbreak\mathit{Act}^{T},\allowbreak\rightarrow^{T})
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{S}\cap\mathit{Act}_{i}^{T}=\emptyset
. The
quotient of
T
and
S
, denoted by
T\backslash\!\backslash S
, is a specification
(Q^{T}\times Q^{S}\cup\{u,e\},(q_{0}^{T},q_{0}^{S}),\mathit{Act},\allowbreak\rightarrow)
where
u
is the universal state,
e
the inconsistent state,
\mathit{Act}=\mathit{Act}_{i}\uplus\mathit{Act}_{o}
with
\mathit{Act}_{i}=\mathit{Act}_{i}^{T}\cup\mathit{Act}_{o}^{S}
and
\mathit{Act}_{o}=\mathit{Act}_{o}^{T}\setminus\mathit{Act}_{o}^{S}\cup\mathit{Act}_{i}^{S}\setminus\mathit{Act}_{i}^{T}
, and
\rightarrow
is defined as

1.

(q_{1}^{T},q_{1}^{S})\xlongrightarrow{a}(q_{2}^{T},q_{2}^{S})
if
a\in\mathit{Act}^{S}\cap\mathit{Act}^{T}
,
q_{1}^{T}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!T}q_{2}^{T}
, and
q_{1}^{S}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!S}q_{2}^{S}

2.

(q^{T},q_{1}^{S})\xlongrightarrow{a}(q^{T},q_{2}^{S})
if
a\in\mathit{Act}^{S}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{T}
,
q^{T}\in Q^{T}
, and
q_{1}^{S}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!S}q_{2}^{S}

3.

(q_{1}^{T},q^{S})\xlongrightarrow{a}(q_{2}^{T},q^{S})
if
a\in\mathit{Act}^{T}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{S}
,
q^{S}\in Q^{S}
, and
q_{1}^{T}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!T}q_{2}^{T}

4.

(q_{1}^{T},q_{1}^{S})\xlongrightarrow{d}(q_{2}^{T},q_{2}^{S})
if
d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
q_{1}^{T}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!T}q_{2}^{T}
, and
q_{1}^{S}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}q_{2}^{S}

5.

(q^{T},q^{S})\xlongrightarrow{a}u
if
a\in\mathit{Act}_{o}^{S}
,
q^{T}\in Q^{T}
, and
q^{S}\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!S}

6.

(q^{T},q^{S})\xlongrightarrow{d}u
if
d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
q^{T}\in Q^{T}
, and
q^{S}\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}

7.

(q^{T},q^{S})\xlongrightarrow{a}e
if
a\in\mathit{Act}_{o}^{S}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{T}
,
q^{T}\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!T}
, and
q^{S}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!S}

8.

u\xlongrightarrow{a}u
if
a\in\mathit{Act}\cup\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

9.

e\xlongrightarrow{a}e
if
a\in\mathit{Act}_{i}

In this definition,
u
and
e
are fresh states such that
u
is universal (allows arbitrary behavior) and
e
is inconsistent (no output-controllable behavior can satisfy it). State
e
disallows progress of time and has no output transitions. The universal state guarantees nothing about the behavior of its implementations (thus any refinement with a suitable alphabet is possible), and dually the inconsistent state allows no implementations.

The first four rules are part of the standard rules of parallel composition, see Definition
15
. Rules 5 and 6 capture the situation where
S
does not allow a particular output action or delay, respectively, so the parallel composition of
S
and the quotient also does not allow this to happen. Therefore, it technically does not matter what the quotient does after performing these transitions, hence they go to the universal state
u
. Rule 7 captures the situation that an output shared between
S
and
T
as causes a problem in the refinement
S\leq T
as
T
is blocking the output. Thus the quotient, representing the missing component put into parallel composition with
S
, needs to block
S
from performing this output action. But the output action has become an input action in the quotient, so we redirect this output to the error state to ‘memorize’ this problem. Finally, rules 8 and 9 simply express what we mean by universal and error state, respectively.

Theorem
10
states that the proposed quotient operator has exactly the property that it is dual of structural composition with regards to refinement.

Theorem 10
.

For any two specifications
S
and
T
such that the quotient
T\backslash\!\backslash S
is defined, and for any implementation
X
over the same alphabet as
T\backslash\!\backslash S
, we have that
S\parallel X
is defined and
S\parallel X\leq T
iff
X\leq T\backslash\!\backslash S
.

The proof of Theorem
10
can be found in Appendix
8.4
.

Quotienting for TIOA is defined in the following way.

Definition 19
.

Given specification automata
S=(\mathit{Loc}^{S},l_{0}^{S},\mathit{Act}^{S},\mathit{Clk}^{S},E^{S},\mathit{Inv}^{S})
and

. The

and

is the universal state,

1.

((l_{1}^{T},l_{1}^{S}),a,\varphi^{T}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Inv}(l_{2}^{T})[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{T}}}\wedge\varphi^{S}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Inv}(l_{1}^{S})}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathit{Inv}(l_{2}^{S})[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{S}}},c^{T}\cup c^{S},(l_{2}^{T},l_{2}^{S}))\in E

Compared to definitions of the quotient for TIOAs in our previous works, we made several changes to correct minor mistakes. 1) Location invariants in the quotient are simply
\mathbf{T}
, hence the location invariants of the specifications
S
and

are now included in the transitions of the quotient. For example, rule 1 captures rule 1 of the quotient for TIOTS (Definition
18
) where transitions are both possible in

and

. A transition is possible when the guard is satisfied, captured by the quotient definitions in previous works,
and
the updated valuation satisfies the target location’s invariant, see Definition
3
of the semantics. 2) We resolved a potential nondeterminism caused by the combination of rules 4 and 8, i.e., in Definition
19
for any given state either the edge emanating from rule 4 is enabled or the one from rule 8, or none, but never both. 3) Similarly, we resolved a potential nondeterminism caused by the combination of rules 4 and 5. 4) Rule 7 is added to ensure that the quotient is actually input enabled by construction for the new input action

that are generated by rule 7 from Definition

. As can be seen from this example, there are two potential problems that can result in errors for the university specification: it can take too long (

Observe that
\llbracket T\backslash\!\backslash S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\backslash\!\backslash\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
have different state and action sets. For example,
\llbracket T\backslash\!\backslash S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
has a set of error states
\{(l_{e},v)\mid v\in[\mathit{Clk}^{T\backslash\!\backslash S}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]\}
, while
\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\backslash\!\backslash\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
only has a single error state
e
. Or
\llbracket T\backslash\!\backslash S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
contains the input action
i_{\mathit{new}}
which
\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\backslash\!\backslash\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
lacks. This makes relating the quotient for TIOTS and TIOA much more tedious that those theorems in previous sections. Therefore, we have to use bisimulation, Definition
20
, in the following main theorem, Theorem
11
, that lifts all the results from timed input/output transition systems to the symbolic representation level
7
7

7

In previous works, the bisimulation was replaced by an equation sign, which is technically incorrect.
.

Definition 20
.

Given specifications
S=(Q^{S},q_{0}^{S},\mathit{Act}^{S},\rightarrow^{S})
and
T=(Q^{T},q_{0}^{T},\allowbreak\mathit{Act}^{T},\allowbreak\rightarrow^{T})
.
S
and
T
are
bisimilar
, denoted by
S\simeq T
, iff there exists a bisimulation relation
R\subseteq Q^{S}\times Q^{T}
containing
(q_{0}^{S},q_{0}^{T})
such that for each pair of states
(s,t)\in R
it holds that

1.

whenever
s\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
for some
s^{\prime}\in Q^{S}
and
a\in\mathit{Act}^{S}\cap\mathit{Act}^{T}
, then
t\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!T}t^{\prime}
and
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in R
for some
t^{\prime}\in Q^{T}

2.

whenever
s\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
for some
s^{\prime}\in Q^{S}
and
a\in\mathit{Act}^{S}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{T}
, then
(s^{\prime},t)\in R

3.

whenever
t\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!T}t^{\prime}
for some
t^{\prime}\in Q^{T}
and
a\in\mathit{Act}^{T}\cap\mathit{Act}^{S}
, then
s\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
and
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in R
for some
s^{\prime}\in Q^{S}

4.

whenever
t\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!T}t^{\prime}
for some
t^{\prime}\in Q^{T}
and
a\in\mathit{Act}^{T}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{S}
, then
(s,t^{\prime})\in R

5.

whenever
s\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
for some
s^{\prime}\in Q^{S}
and
d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, then
t\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!T}t^{\prime}
and
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in R
for some
t^{\prime}\in Q^{T}

6.

whenever
t\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!T}t^{\prime}
for some
t^{\prime}\in Q^{T}
and
d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, then
s\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
and
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in R
for some
s^{\prime}\in Q^{S}

Two specification automata
A
and
B
are bisimilar, denoted by
A\simeq B
, iff
\llbracket A\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\simeq\llbracket B\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
.

Theorem 11
.

Given specification automata
S=(\mathit{Loc}^{S},l_{0}^{S},\mathit{Act}^{S},\mathit{Clk}^{S},E^{S},\mathit{Inv}^{S})
and
T=(\mathit{Loc}^{T},l_{0}^{T},\allowbreak\mathit{Act}^{T},\mathit{Clk}^{T},E^{T},\allowbreak\mathit{Inv}^{T})
where
\mathit{Act}_{o}^{S}\cap\mathit{Act}_{i}^{T}=\emptyset
. Then
(\llbracket T\backslash\!\backslash S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}\simeq(\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\backslash\!\backslash\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}})^{\Delta}
.

The proof of Theorem
11
can be found in Appendix
8.4
.

7
Concluding Remarks

We have proposed a
complete and fully proven
game-based specification theory for timed systems, in which we distinguish between a component and the environment in which it is used. Our contribution is a game-based approach to support both refinement, consistency checking, logical and structural composition, and quotient.

In the future one could extend our model with (discrete) variables to ease the modeling of cyber-physical systems. This was already suggested by Berendsen and Vaandrager in
berendsen_compositional_2008
, but only for structural composition.

One could also investigate whether our approach can be used to perform scheduling of timed systems (see
alfaro_accelerated_2007
;
henzinger_embedded_2006
;
deng_scheduling_1997
for examples). For example, the quotient operation could perhaps be used to synthesize a scheduler for such problem.

In this paper, we ignored the notion of time divergence as defined in
alfaro_element_2003
. In Section
3
we observed that to verify whether an implementation has time divergence, we need to analyze it in the context of an environment to form a closed-system, as an environment could both ensure or prevent the diverging of time. It would be interesting to investigate whether one could investigate time divergence in a compositional manner.

Figure 14
:
Screenshot of the GUI of Ecdar 2.4

Finally our methodology is being implemented in the open-source tool ECDAR
8
8

8

http://ecdar.net
. Conjunction, composition, and quotienting are simple product constructions allowing for consistency checking to be solved using the zone-based algorithms for synthesizing winning strategies in timed games
maler_synthesis_1995
;
cassez_efficident_2005
. Refinement between specifications can be checked using a variant of the pre-existing efficient game-based algorithm
Bulychev_efficient_2009
.
A previous version of the tool was closed-source, contained a few issues and did not implement some of the features, like quotient. The version currently in development contains all features, is thoroughly tested and will support Boolean variables. Besides the implementation of the algorithms, we are also working an on graphical user interface (GUI) to model systems. Figure
14
shows a screenshot of the current state of the GUI.

Acknowledgments

This study was funded by the European Research Council (ERC) Advance grant LASSO, the Villum Investigator grant S4OS, and the Digital Research Center Denmark (DIREC) Bridge grant ‘Verifiable and Safe AI for Autonomous Systems’.

Declarations

The authors have no competing interests to declare that are relevant to the content of this article.

8
Proofs

This section contains all the proofs not included in the main text of the paper. We will repeat the theorem for clarity before actually providing the proof.

8.1
Omitted proofs of Section
3

Theorem
1
.

Every locally consistent specification is consistent in the sense of Definition
8
.

Proof.

Let us begin with defining an auxiliary function
\delta
which chooses a delay for every state
s
in a
locally consistent
specification
S
:

\delta{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(s)}=\begin{cases}d&\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}the infimum $d$} such that }s\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}\text{ and }\exists o!:s^{\prime}\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!S}\\
+\infty&\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}otherwise}}\end{cases}

Note that
since
s
allows independent progress, it always hold that
s\xlongrightarrow{\delta(s)}{}^{\!\!S}
.

\delta
is time additive in the following sense: if
s\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
and
d\leq\delta(s)
then
\delta(s^{\prime})+d=\delta(s)
,
which is
due to time additivity of
\rightarrow^{S}
, and local consistency of
S
.

We want to show for an arbitrary locally consistent specifications
S
that it has an implementation. This can be shown by synthesizing an implementation
P=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{S},s_{0},\mathit{Act}^{S}},\rightarrow^{P})
, where
\rightarrow^{P}
is the largest transition relation generated by the following rules:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}\xlongrightarrow{i?}{}^{\!\!P}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }s\xlongrightarrow{i?}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}\wedge i?\in\mathit{Act}_{i}^{S}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!P}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }s\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}\wedge o!\in\mathit{Act}_{o}^{S}\wedge\delta(s)=0

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!P}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\mbox{ if }s\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}\wedge d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\wedge d\leq\delta(s)

Since
P
only takes a subset of transitions of
S
, the determinism of
S
implies determinism of
P
. The transition relation of
P
is time-additive due to time additivity of
\rightarrow^{S}
and of
\delta
. It is also time-reflexive due to the last rule (
0\leq\delta(s)
for every state
s
and
\rightarrow^{S}
was time reflexive). So
P
is a TIOTS.

The new transition relation is also input enabled as it inherits of input transitions from
S
, which was input enabled. So
P
is a specification. The second rule guarantees that outputs are urgent (
by construction

P
only outputs when no further delays are possible). Moreover
P
observes independent progress. Consider a state
s
in
P
. Then if
\delta(s)=+\infty
clearly
s
can delay indefinitely. If
\delta(s)
is finite, then by definition of
\delta
and of
P
, the state
s
can delay and hence produce an output. Thus
P
is an implementation in the sense of Definition
5
.

Now an unsurprising coinductive argument shows that the following relation
R\subseteq Q^{S}\times Q^{S}
witnesses
P\mathbin{\leq}S
:

R=\left\{(s,s)\mid s\in Q^{S}\right\}.

∎

Theorem
2
.

Any locally consistent specification
S
refining an implementation
P
is an implementation as per Definition
5
.

Proof.

Observe first that
S
is already locally consistent, so all its states warrant independent progress. We only need to argue that it satisfies output urgency. Without loss of generality, assume that
S
only contains states which are reachable by (sequences of) discrete or timed transitions.

If
S
only contains reachable states, every state of
S
has to be related to some state of
P
in a relation
R
witnessing
S\leq P
(output and delay transitions need to be matched in the refinement; input transitions also need to be matched as
P
is input enabled and
S
is deterministic). This can be argued for using a standard, though slightly lengthy argument, by formalizing reachable states as a fixpoint of a monotonic operator.

Now that we know that every state of
S
is related to some state of
P
consider an arbitrary
s\in Q^{S}
and let
p\in Q^{P}
be such that
(s,p)\in R
. Then if
s\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
for some state
s^{\prime}\in Q^{S}
and an output
o!\in\mathit{Act}_{o}^{S}
, it must be that also
p\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!P}p^{\prime}
for some state
p^{\prime}\in Q^{P}
(and
(s^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in R
).
But since
P
is an implementation, its outputs must be urgent, so
p\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!P}
for all
d>0
, and consequently
s\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}
for all
d>0
. We have shown that all states of
S
have urgent outputs (if any) and thus
S
is an implementation.
∎

8.2
Omitted proofs of Section
4

Theorem
4
.

A specification
S=(Q,s_{0},\mathit{Act},\rightarrow)
is consistent iff
s_{0}\in\mathrm{cons}
.

Proof.

First, assume that
s_{0}\in\mathrm{cons}^{S}
. Show that
S
is consistent in the sense of Definition
8
. In a similar fashion to the proof of Theorem
1
we first postulate existence of a function
\delta
, which chooses a delay and an output for every consistent state
s
:

\delta{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(s)}=\begin{cases}d&\text{if }\exists s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{cons}^{S}:\text{ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}the infimum $d$} such that }s\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}\\
&\text{and }\exists o!:s^{\prime}\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime\prime}\\
+\infty&\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}otherwise}}\end{cases}

Note that
\delta
is time additive in the following sense: if
s\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
and
d\leq\delta(s)
then
\delta(s^{\prime})+d=\delta(s)
, which is due to
time additivity of
\rightarrow^{S}
and the fact that
\mathrm{cons}^{S}
is a fixpoint of
\Theta^{S}
.

We show this by constructing an implementation
P=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q^{S},s_{0},\mathit{Act}^{S}},\rightarrow^{P})

where
the transition relation is the largest relation generated by the following rules:

1.

s\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!P}s^{\prime}
iff
s\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cons}^{S}
and
\delta_{s}=0
,

2.

s\xlongrightarrow{i?}{}^{\!\!P}s^{\prime}
iff
s\xlongrightarrow{i?}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
,

3.

s\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!P}s^{\prime}
iff
s\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
and
d\leq\delta_{s}
.

Observe that the construction of
P
is essentially identical to the one in the proof of Theorem
1
above. It can be argued in almost the same way as in the above proof, that
P
satisfies the axioms of TIO
TS
s and is an implementation. Here one has to use the definition of
\Theta^{S}
in order to see that the side condition in the first rule, that is
s^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cons}^{S}
, does not introduce a violation of independent progress.

It remains to argue that
P\mathbin{\leq}S
. This is done by arguing that the following relation
R

R=\left\{(p,s)\in Q^{S}\times Q^{S}\mid p=s\right\}

witnesses the refinement of
S
by
P
.

Consider now the other direction. Assume that
S
is consistent and show that
s_{0}\in\mathrm{cons}^{S}
. In the following we write that a state
s
is consistent meaning that a specification would be consistent if
s
was the initial state. Let
X=\{s\in Q^{S}\mid s\text{ is consistent}\}
. It suffices to show that
X
is a post-fixed point of
\Theta^{S}
, thus
X\subseteq\Theta^{S}(X)
(then
s_{0}\in X=\mathrm{cons}^{S}
).

Since
s
is consistent, let us consider an implementation
P
and a state
p
such that
p\mathbin{\leq}s
. We will show that
s\in\Theta^{S}(X)
. Consider an arbitrary
d\geq 0
and the first disjunct in the definition of
\Theta^{S}
. If
p\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!P}p^{d}
then also
s\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!S}s^{d}
and
p^{d}\mathbin{\leq}s^{d}
, so
s^{d}\in X
. Consider an arbitrary input
i?
such that
s^{d}\xlongrightarrow{i?}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
. Then also
p^{d}\xlongrightarrow{i?}{}^{\!\!P}p^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime}\mathbin{\leq}s^{\prime}
(by satisfaction). But then
s^{\prime}\in X
. So by the first disjunct of definition of
\Theta^{S}
we have that
s\in\Theta^{S}(X)
.

If
p\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!P}
for our fixed value of
d
, then by independent progress of
p
there exists a
d_{\mathrm{max}}<d
such that
p\xlongrightarrow{d_{\mathrm{max}}}{}^{\!\!P}p^{\prime}
for some
p^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime}\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!P}p^{\prime\prime}
for some
p^{\prime\prime}
and some output
o!
. By
p\mathbin{\leq}s
there also exist
s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime\prime}
such that
s\xlongrightarrow{d_{\mathrm{max}}}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!S}s^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover
p^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\leq}s^{\prime\prime}
, so
s^{\prime\prime}\in X
, which by the second disjunct in the definition of
\Theta^{S}
implies that
s\in\Theta^{S}(X)
.

So we conclude that
X
is a fixpoint of
\Theta^{S}
. Since
s_{0}
is consistent by assumption, then
s_{0}\in X\subseteq\mathrm{cons}^{S}
.
∎

Theorem
5
.

For a consistent specification
S
,
S^{\Delta}
is locally consistent and
\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{mod}}=\llbracket S^{\Delta}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{mod}}
.

Proof.

We first proof that
S^{\Delta}
is locally consistent. From Definitions
9
and
5
of local consistency and implementation, respectively, it follows that we have to show that
\forall q\in Q^{S^{\Delta}}
: either
\forall d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}:q\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!P}
or
\exists d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\exists o!\in\mathit{Act}_{o}
s.t.
q\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!P}q^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!P}
. From Definition
12
of adversarial pruning it follows that
Q^{S^{\Delta}}=\mathrm{cons}
.

Consider a state
q\in\mathrm{cons}
. From the definition of
\Theta
, it follows that
q\in\overline{\mathrm{err}(\overline{\mathrm{cons}})}
and
q\in\{q_{1}\in Q\mid\forall d\geq 0:[\forall q_{2}\in Q:q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d}q_{2}\Rightarrow q_{2}\in\mathrm{cons}\wedge\forall i?\in\mathit{Act}_{i}:\exists q_{3}\in\mathrm{cons}:q_{2}\xlongrightarrow{i?}q_{3}]\ \vee[\exists d^{\prime}\leq d\wedge\exists q_{2},q_{3}\in\mathrm{cons}\wedge\exists o!\in\mathit{Act}_{o}:q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d^{\prime}}q_{2}\wedge q_{2}\xlongrightarrow{o!}q_{3}\wedge\forall i?\in\mathit{Act}_{i}:\exists q_{4}\in\mathrm{cons}:q_{2}\xlongrightarrow{i?}q_{4}]\}
. In case that the condition
[\exists d^{\prime}\leq d\wedge\exists q_{2},q_{3}\in\mathrm{cons}\wedge\exists o!\in\mathit{Act}_{o}:q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d^{\prime}}q_{2}\wedge q_{2}\xlongrightarrow{o!}q_{3}\wedge\forall i?\in\mathit{Act}_{i}:\exists q_{4}\in\mathrm{cons}:q_{2}\xlongrightarrow{i?}q_{4}]
holds for some
d
, then it follows immediately that
q
allows independent progress. In the other case, i.e., there does not exists a
d
such that
[\exists d^{\prime}\leq d\wedge\exists q_{2},q_{3}\in\mathrm{cons}\wedge\exists o!\in\mathit{Act}_{o}:q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d^{\prime}}q_{2}\wedge q_{2}\xlongrightarrow{o!}q_{3}\wedge\forall i?\in\mathit{Act}_{i}:\exists q_{4}\in\mathrm{cons}:q_{2}\xlongrightarrow{i?}q_{4}]
holds, it follows from the fact that
q\in\overline{\mathrm{err}(\overline{\mathrm{cons}})}
and Definition
11
that
\forall d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}:q\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!P}
, thus allowing independent progress.

We now show that
\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{mod}}=\llbracket S^{\Delta}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{mod}}
. From Definition
7
it follows that
\llbracket S\rrbracket_{\mathrm{mod}}=\llbracket S^{\Delta}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{mod}}
iff for all implementations
P
it holds that
P\leq S\Leftrightarrow P\leq S^{\Delta}
.

(
P\leq S\Rightarrow P\leq S^{\Delta}
)
Consider an implementation
P
such that
P\leq S
. This implies from Definition
6
of refinement that there exists a relation
R\subseteq Q^{P}\times Q^{S}
witnessing the refinement. We will arguing that for any pair
(p,s)\in R
it holds that
s\in\mathrm{cons}
.

For this, consider the controllable predecessor operator
\pi
and
\pi(\mathrm{imerr})
to understand what it exactly calculates with respect to the definition of a consistent specification. A state
q\in\pi(\mathrm{imerr})
is either directly an error state or it can first delay followed by an input action to reach an error state
without
encountering an output action preventing it reaching an error state. With other words, no implementation can prevent state
q
from reaching an error state.

Now, denote
\pi^{n}(\mathrm{err})
the
n
-th iteration of the fixed-point calculation, i.e.,
\pi^{1}(\mathrm{imerr})=\pi(\mathrm{imerr})
,
\pi^{2}(\mathrm{imerr})=\pi(\pi(\mathrm{imerr}))
, etc. Following the above reasoning about the effect of
\pi
on the reachability of error states, we can formulate the following fixed-point invariant: for each
n
and
q\in\pi^{n}(\mathrm{err})
, there does not exists an implementation preventing
q
from reaching an error state. Once the fixed-point
\mathrm{incons}=\pi(\mathrm{incons})=\pi^{N}(\mathrm{imerr})
for some
N
is reached, we know for all
q\in\overline{\mathrm{incons}}
that it cannot reach the fixed-point
\mathrm{incons}
because either
\mathrm{incons}
is just simply unreachable by any means or an implementation can prevent it from reaching it.

Consider a pair
(p,s)\in R
where
s\in\mathrm{incons}
. This means that specification
S
cannot be prevented from reaching an error state
s^{\prime}
. If we follow this path, we end up with pair
(p^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in R
. Now,
s^{\prime}
is an error state, which either cannot progress time indefinitely and do an output. But since
p^{\prime}
is a state from an implementation
P
, it has the independent progress property. Therefore, once the specification wants to do an output or (indefinite) delay, the second or third property from Definition
6
is violated. Therefore, we can conclude that for pair
(p,s)\in R
,
s\notin\mathrm{incons}
, i.e.,
s\in\mathrm{cons}
. As the argument above does not rely on a specific state
s
in
S
, it holds for all states
s\in Q^{S}
.

Now, we effectively have that
R\subseteq Q^{P}\times\mathrm{cons}
, thus it follows from Definition
12
of adversarial pruning that
R
is also a relation witnessing the refinement
P\leq S^{\Delta}
. As we considered an arbitrarily implementation
P
refining
S
, it holds for all implementations
P
refining
S
. Therefore, we conclude that
P\leq S\Leftarrow P\leq S^{\Delta}
.

(
P\leq S\Leftarrow P\leq S^{\Delta}
)
This case follows directly from the construction of
S^{\Delta}
and the fact that
\mathrm{cons}\subseteq Q^{S}
, i.e., for all implementations
P
that refine
S^{\Delta}
, the binary relation
R\subseteq Q^{P}\times\mathrm{cons}
also witnesses the refinement of
P
and
S
.

∎

Lemma
2
.

For two specifications
S
,
T
, and their states
s
and
t
, respectively, if there exists an implementation
P
and its state
p
such that simultaneously
p\mathbin{\leq}s
and
p\mathbin{\leq}t
then
(s,t)\in\mathrm{cons}^{S\wedge T}
.

Proof.

This is shown by arguing that the following set
X
of states of
S\wedge T
is a postfixed point of
\Theta
(then
(s,t)\in X\subseteq\Theta(X)\subseteq\mathrm{cons}^{S\wedge T}
):

X=\{(s,t)\mid\exists P:\exists p\in Q^{P}:p\mathbin{\leq}s\land p\mathbin{\leq}t\}.

This is done by checking that
X\subseteq\Theta(X)
. Take
(s,t)\in X
, show that
(s,t)\in\Theta(X)
. So consider an arbitrary
d_{0}\geq 0
. We know that there exists state
p
such that
p\mathbin{\leq}s
and
p\mathbin{\leq}t
. Since
p
is a state of an implementation it guarantees independent progress, so there exists a delay
d^{p}
such that
p\xlongrightarrow{d^{p}}{}^{\!\!P}p^{\prime}
for some state
p^{\prime}
. Now the proof is split in two cases, proceeding by coinduction.

•

d^{p}\leq d_{0}
is used to show that
(s,t)\in\Theta(X)
using a standard argument with the second disjunct in definition of
\Theta
(namely that
p
can delay and output leading to a refinement of successors of
s
and
t
, which again will be in
X
).

•

d^{p}>d_{0}
is used to show that
(s,t)\in\Theta(X)
using the same kind of argument with the first disjunct in the definition of
\Theta
(namely that then
p
can delay
d_{0}
time and by refinement for any input transition it can advanced to a state refining successors of
s
and
t
, which are in
X
).

∎

Lemma
3
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{i}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset\wedge\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{i}^{2}=\emptyset
. Then
Q^{\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=Q^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
and
q_{0}^{\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=q_{0}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
.

Proof.

For brevity, we write
X=\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
,
Y=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and
\mathit{Clk}=\mathit{Clk}^{1}\uplus\mathit{Clk}^{2}
in the rest of this proof. Following Definition
3
of semantic of a TIOA, Definition
12
of adversarial pruning, Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS, and Definition
14
of the conjunction for TIOA, the set of states of
X
is
Q^{X}=(\mathit{Loc}^{1}\times\mathit{Loc}^{2})\times[\mathit{Clk}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]=\mathit{Loc}^{1}\times\mathit{Loc}^{2}\times[\mathit{Clk}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
and the set of states of
Y
is
Q^{Y}=(\mathit{Loc}^{1}\times[\mathit{Clk}^{1}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}])\times(\mathit{Loc}^{2}\times[\mathit{Clk}^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}])=\mathit{Loc}^{1}\times\mathit{Loc}^{2}\times[\mathit{Clk}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
. Therefore,
Q^{X}=Q^{Y}
. Furthermore, it now also follows immediately from the same definitions that
q_{0}^{X}=q_{0}^{Y}
, as none of these definitions alter the initial location of a TIOA or initial state of a TIOTS.

∎

Lemma
4
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{i}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset\wedge\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{i}^{2}=\emptyset
. Denote
X=\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
Y=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and let
d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q^{X}\cap Q^{Y}
. Then
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
if and only if
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
.

Proof.

First, from Lemma
3
it follows that
Q^{X}=Q^{Y}
. Consider a delay
d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. For brevity, in the rest of this proof we write
\mathit{Clk}=\mathit{Clk}^{1}\uplus\mathit{Clk}^{2}
, and
u^{1}
and
u^{2}
to indicate the part of a valuation
u
of only the clocks of
A^{1}
and
A^{2}
, respectively.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Assume that
\exists q_{1},q_{2}\in Q^{X}
such that
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
. From Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA it follows that
q_{1}=(l,v)
,
q_{2}=(l,v+d)
,
l\in\mathit{Loc}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
,
v\in[\mathit{Clk}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
,
v+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l)
, and
\forall d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},d^{\prime}<d:v+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l)
. From Definition
14
of the conjunction for TIOA it follows that
l=(l^{1},l^{2})
,
l^{1}\in\mathit{Loc}^{1}
,
l^{2}\in\mathit{Loc}^{2}
, and
\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l)=\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l^{1})\wedge\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
. Therefore,
v+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l^{1})\wedge\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
, and thus
v+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l^{1})
and
v+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
. Similarly,
v+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l^{1})\wedge\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
, and thus
v+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l^{1})
and
v+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
. Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it follows that
v^{1}+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l^{1})
,
v^{2}+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
,
v^{1}+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l^{1})
, and
v^{2}+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
. Now, from Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA, it follows that
(l^{1},v^{1})\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(l^{1},v^{1}+d)
and
(l^{2},v^{2})\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(l^{2},v^{2}+d)
. Finally, from Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS, if follows that
(l^{1},v^{1},l^{2},v^{2})\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!Y}(l^{1},v^{1}+d,l^{2},v^{2}+d)
. Again by using that
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, we can rewrite the states:
(l^{1},v^{1},l^{2},v^{2})=(l^{1},l^{2},v)=q_{1}
and
(l^{1},v^{1}+d,l^{2},v^{2}+d)=(l^{1},l^{2},v+d)=q_{2}
. Thus
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
Assume that
\exists q_{1},q_{2}\in Q^{Y}
such that
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
. From Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
q_{1}=(q_{1}^{1},q_{1}^{2})
,
q_{2}=(q_{2}^{1},q_{2}^{2})
,
q_{1}^{1},q_{2}^{1}\in Q^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
,
q_{1}^{2},q_{2}^{2}\in Q^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
,
q_{1}^{1}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}q_{2}^{1}
, and
q_{1}^{2}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}q_{2}^{2}
. From Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA it follows that for
i=1,2
:
q_{1}^{i}=(l^{i},v^{i})
,
q_{2}^{i}=(l^{i},v^{i}+d)
,
l^{i}\in\mathit{Loc}^{i}
,
v^{i}\in[\mathit{Clk}^{i}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
,
v^{i}+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{i}(l^{i})
, and
\forall d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},d^{\prime}<d:v+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{i}(l^{i})
. Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it follows that for
i=1,2
:
v+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{i}(l^{i})
and
v+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{i}(l^{i})
. Now, from Definition
14
it follows that
\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l^{1},l^{2})=\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l^{1})\wedge\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
. Thus we know that
v+d\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}((l^{1},l^{2}))
and
v+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}((l^{1},l^{2}))
. Therefore, using Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA, it follows that
(l^{1},l^{2},v)\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!X}(l^{1},l^{2},v+d)
. Again by using that
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, we can rewrite the states:
(l^{1},l^{2},v)=(l^{1},v^{1},l^{2},v^{2})=q_{1}
and
(l^{1},l^{2},v+d)=(l^{1},v^{1}+d,l^{2},v^{2}+d)=q_{2}
. Thus
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
.

As the analysis above holds for any chosen
d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, it holds for all
d
. This concludes the proof.

∎

Lemma
5
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{i}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset\wedge\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{i}^{2}=\emptyset
. Denote
X=\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
Y=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and let
a\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}^{2}
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q^{X}\cap Q^{Y}
. Then
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
if and only if
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
.

Proof.

First, from Lemma
3
it follows that
Q^{X}=Q^{Y}
. For brevity, in the rest of this proof we write
\mathit{Clk}=\mathit{Clk}^{1}\uplus\mathit{Clk}^{2}
, and
v^{1}
and
v^{2}
to indicate the part of a valuation
v
of only the clocks of
A^{1}
and
A^{2}
, respectively.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Assume a transition
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
in
X
. Following Definition
3
of the semantic, it follows that there exists an edge
(l_{1},a,\varphi,c,l_{2})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
with
q_{1}^{X}=(l_{1},v_{1})
,
q_{2}^{X}=(l_{2},v_{2})
,
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathit{Loc}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
,
v_{1},v_{2}\in[\mathit{Clk}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
,
v_{1}\models\varphi
,
v_{2}=v_{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}
, and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}(l_{2})
.

From Definition
14
of the conjunction for TIOA it follows that
(l_{1}^{1},a,\varphi^{1},c^{1},l_{2}^{1})
is an edge in
A^{1}
and
(l_{1}^{2},a,\varphi^{2},c^{2},l_{2}^{2})
in
A^{2}
,
l_{1}=(l_{1}^{1},l_{1}^{2})
,
l_{2}=(l_{2}^{1},l_{2}^{2})
,
\varphi=\varphi^{1}\wedge\varphi^{2}
,
c=c^{1}\cup c^{2}
. Since
v_{1}\models\varphi
, it holds that
v_{1}\models\varphi^{1}
and
v_{1}\models\varphi^{2}
. Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it holds that
v_{1}^{1}\models\varphi^{1}
and
v_{1}^{2}\models\varphi^{2}
. Also, since
v_{2}=v_{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}
, it holds that
v_{2}^{1}=v_{1}^{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}
and
v_{2}^{2}=v_{1}^{2}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{2}}
. Finally, because
\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{2})=\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})\wedge\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{2}^{2})
(see Definition
14
) and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{2})
, it follows that
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{2}^{2})
. Since
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it follows that
v_{2}^{1}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
and
v_{2}^{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{2}^{2})
.

Combining all the information about
A^{1}
, we have that
(l_{1}^{1},a,\varphi^{1},c^{1},l_{2}^{1})
is an edge in
A^{1}
,
v_{1}^{1}\models\varphi^{1}
,
v_{2}^{1}=v_{1}^{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}
, and
v_{2}^{1}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
. Therefore, from Definition
3
it follows that
(l_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{1})\xrightarrow{a}(l_{2}^{1},v_{2}^{1})
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Combining all the information about
A^{2}
, we have that
(l_{1}^{2},a,\varphi^{2},c^{2},l_{2}^{2})
is an edge in
A^{2}
,
v_{1}^{2}\models\varphi^{2}
,
v_{2}^{2}=v_{1}^{2}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{2}}
, and
v_{2}^{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{2}^{2})
. Therefore, from Definition
3
it follows that
(l_{1}^{2},v_{1}^{2})\xrightarrow{a}(l_{2}^{2},v_{2}^{2})
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
.

Now, from Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
((l_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{1}),(l_{1}^{2},v_{1}^{2}))\xrightarrow{a}((l_{2}^{1},v_{2}^{1}),\allowbreak(l_{2}^{2},v_{2}^{2}))
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, we can rearrange the states into
((l_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{1}),(l_{1}^{2},v_{1}^{2}))=((l_{1}^{1},l_{1}^{2}),v_{1})=q_{1}^{X}
and
((l_{2}^{1},v_{2}^{1}),(l_{2}^{2},v_{2}^{2}))=((l_{2}^{1},l_{2}^{2}),v_{2})=q_{2}^{X}
. Thus,
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}=Y
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
Assume a transition
q_{1}^{Y}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{Y}
in
Y
. From Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
q_{1}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
q_{1}^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
in
\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
,
q_{1}^{Y}=(q_{1}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}},q_{1}^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}})
, and
q_{2}^{Y}=(q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}},q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}})
. From Definition
3
of semantic it follows that there exists an edge
(l_{1}^{1},a,\varphi^{1},c^{1},l_{2}^{1})\in E^{1}
with
q_{1}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=(l_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{1})
,
q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=(l_{2}^{1},v_{2}^{1})
,
l_{1}^{1},l_{2}^{1}\in\mathit{Loc}^{1}
,
v_{1}^{1},v_{2}^{1}\in[\mathit{Clk}^{1}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
,
v_{1}^{1}\models\varphi^{1}
,
v_{2}^{1}=v_{1}^{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}
, and
v_{2}^{1}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
. Similarly, it follows from the same definition that there exists an edge
(l_{1}^{2},a,\varphi^{2},c^{2},l_{2}^{2})\in E^{2}
with
q_{1}^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=(l_{1}^{2},v_{1}^{2})
,
q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=(l_{2}^{2},v_{2}^{2})
,
l_{1}^{2},l_{2}^{2}\in\mathit{Loc}^{2}
,
v_{1}^{2},v_{2}^{2}\in[\mathit{Clk}^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
,
v_{1}^{2}\models\varphi^{2}
,
v_{2}^{2}=v_{1}^{2}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{2}}
, and
v_{2}^{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{2}^{2})
.

Now, from Definition
14
of the conjunction for TIOA, it follows that there exists an edge
((l_{1}^{1},l_{1}^{2}),a,\varphi^{1}\wedge\varphi^{2},c^{1}\cup c^{2},(l_{2}^{1},l_{2}^{2}))
in
A^{1}\wedge A^{2}
. Let
v_{i},i=1,2
be the valuations that combines the one from
A^{1}
with the one from
A^{2}
, i.e.
\forall r\in\mathit{Clk}^{1}:v_{i}(r)=v_{i}^{1}(r)
and
\forall r\in\mathit{Clk}^{2}:v_{i}(r)=v_{i}^{2}(r)
. Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it holds that
v_{1}\models\varphi^{1}
and
v_{1}\models\varphi^{2}
, thus
v_{1}\models\varphi^{1}\wedge\varphi^{2}
;
v_{2}=v_{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}\cup c^{2}}
; and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{2}^{2})
, thus
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})\wedge\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{2}^{2})
.

From Definition
3
it now follows that
((l_{1}^{1},l_{1}^{2}),v_{1})\xrightarrow{a}((l_{2}^{1},l_{2}^{2}),v_{2})
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, we can rearrange the states into
((l_{1}^{1},l_{1}^{2}),v_{1})=((l_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{1}),(l_{1}^{2},v_{1}^{2}))=q_{1}^{Y}
and
((l_{2}^{1},l_{2}^{2}),v_{2})=((l_{2}^{1},v_{2}^{1}),(l_{2}^{2},v_{2}^{2}))=q_{2}^{Y}
. Thus,
q_{1}^{Y}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{Y}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}=Y
.

∎

Lemma
6
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{i}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset\wedge\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{i}^{2}=\emptyset
. Denote
X=\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
Y=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and let
a\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{2}
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q^{X}\cap Q^{Y}
, where
q_{2}=(l_{2}^{1},l_{2}^{2},v_{2})
. If
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{2})
, then
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
if and only if
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
.

Proof.

First, from Lemma
3
it follows that
Q^{X}=Q^{Y}
. For brevity, in the rest of this proof we write
\mathit{Clk}=\mathit{Clk}^{1}\uplus\mathit{Clk}^{2}
, and
v^{1}
and
v^{2}
to indicate the part of a valuation
v
of only the clocks of
A^{1}
and
A^{2}
, respectively.

(
\Rightarrow
)
Assume a transition
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
in
X
. Following Definition
3
of the semantic, it follows that there exists an edge
(l_{1},a,\varphi,c,l_{2})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
with
q_{1}^{X}=(l_{1},v_{1})
,
q_{2}^{X}=(l_{2},v_{2})
,
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathit{Loc}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
,
v_{1},v_{2}\in[\mathit{Clk}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
,
v_{1}\models\varphi
,
v_{2}=v_{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}
, and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}(l_{2})
.

From Definition
14
of the conjunction for TIOA it follows that
(l_{1}^{1},a,\varphi^{1},c^{1},l_{2}^{1})
is an edge in
A^{1}
,
l_{1}=(l_{1}^{1},l_{1}^{2})
,
l_{2}=(l_{2}^{1},l_{2}^{2})
,
l_{1}^{2}=l_{2}^{2}=l^{2}
,
\varphi=\varphi^{1}
,
c=c^{1}
. Since
v_{1}\models\varphi
and
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it holds that
v_{1}^{1}\models\varphi^{1}
. Also, since
v_{2}=v_{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}
and
c=c^{1}
, it holds that
v_{2}^{1}=v_{1}^{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}
and
v_{2}^{2}=v_{1}^{2}
. Finally, because
\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{2})=\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})\wedge\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
(see Definition
14
) and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{2})
, it follows that
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
9
9

9

So the if condition in the lemma is always satisfied once we know that
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
is a transition in
X
. We formalize this in Corollary
1
.
. Since
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it follows that
v_{2}^{1}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
and
v_{2}^{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
.

Combining all the information about
A^{1}
, we have that
(l_{1}^{1},a,\varphi^{1},c^{1},l_{2}^{1})
is an edge in
A^{1}
,
v_{1}^{1}\models\varphi^{1}
,
v_{2}^{1}=v_{1}^{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}
, and
v_{2}^{1}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
. Therefore, from Definition
3
it follows that
(l_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{1})\xrightarrow{a}(l_{2}^{1},v_{2}^{1})
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Combining all the information about
A^{2}
, we have that
v_{1}^{2}=v_{2}^{2}
and
v_{2}^{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
.

Now, from Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
((l_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{1}),(l^{2},v_{1}^{2}))\xrightarrow{a}((l_{2}^{1},v_{2}^{1}),\allowbreak(l^{2},v_{1}^{2}))
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, we can rearrange the states into
((l_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{1}),(l^{2},v_{1}^{2}))=((l_{1}^{1},l^{2}),v_{1})=q_{1}^{X}
and
((l_{2}^{1},v_{2}^{1}),(l^{2},v_{2}^{2}))=((l_{2}^{1},l^{2}),v_{2})=q_{2}^{X}
. Thus,
q_{1}^{X}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{X}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}=Y
.

(
\Leftarrow
)
Assume a transition
q_{1}^{Y}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{Y}
in
Y
. From Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
q_{1}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
,
q^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}\in Q^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
,
q_{1}^{Y}=(q_{1}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}},q^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}})
, and
q_{2}^{Y}=(q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}},q^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}})
. From Definition
3
of semantic it follows that there exists an edge
(l_{1}^{1},a,\varphi^{1},c^{1},l_{2}^{1})\in E^{1}
with
q_{1}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=(l_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{1})
,
q_{2}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=(l_{2}^{1},v_{2}^{1})
,
l_{1}^{1},l_{2}^{1}\in\mathit{Loc}^{1}
,
v_{1}^{1},v_{2}^{1}\in[\mathit{Clk}^{1}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
,
v_{1}^{1}\models\varphi^{1}
,
v_{2}^{1}=v_{1}^{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}
, and
v_{2}^{1}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
. Similarly, it follows from the same definition that
q^{\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}=(l^{2},v^{2})
,
l^{2}\in\mathit{Loc}^{2}
, and
v^{2}\in[\mathit{Clk}^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
.

Now, from Definition
14
of the conjunction for TIOA, it follows that there exists an edge
((l_{1}^{1},l^{2}),a,\varphi^{1},c^{1},(l_{2}^{1},l^{2}))
in
A^{1}\wedge A^{2}
. Let
v_{i},i=1,2
, be a valuation that combines the one from
A^{1}
with the one from
A^{2}
, i.e.
\forall r\in\mathit{Clk}^{1}:v_{i}(r)=v_{i}^{1}(r)
and
\forall r\in\mathit{Clk}^{2}:v_{i}(r)=v_{i}^{2}(r)
. Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it holds that
v_{1}\models\varphi^{1}
;
v_{2}=v_{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}
with
v_{1}^{2}=v_{2}^{2}
; and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
. As the antecedent states that
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
, it follows that
v^{2}\models\mathit{Inv}(l_{2}^{1})\wedge\mathit{Inv}(l^{2})
.

From Definition
3
it now follows that
((l_{1}^{1},l^{2}),v_{1})\xrightarrow{a}((l_{2}^{1},l^{2}),v_{2})
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
. Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, we can rearrange the states into
((l_{1}^{1},l^{2}),v_{1})=((l_{1}^{1},v_{1}^{1}),(l^{2},v_{1}^{2}))=q_{1}^{Y}
and
((l_{2}^{1},l^{2}),v_{2})=((l_{2}^{1},v_{2}^{1}),(l^{2},v_{2}^{2}))=q_{2}^{Y}
. Thus,
q_{1}^{Y}\xrightarrow{a}q_{2}^{Y}
is a transition in
\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}=Y
.

∎

Corollary
1
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{i}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset\wedge\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{i}^{2}=\emptyset
. Denote
X=\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
Y=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and let
a\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{2}
and
q_{1},q_{2}\in Q^{X}\cap Q^{Y}
. If
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!X}q_{2}
, then
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
.

Proof.

First, from Lemma
3
it follows that
Q^{X}=Q^{Y}
. For brevity, in the rest of this proof we write
\mathit{Clk}=\mathit{Clk}^{1}\uplus\mathit{Clk}^{2}
, and
v^{1}
and
v^{2}
to indicate the part of a valuation
v
of only the clocks of
A^{1}
and
A^{2}
, respectively.

Following Definition
3
of the semantic, it follows that there exists an edge
(l_{1},a,\varphi,c,l_{2})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
with
q_{1}^{X}=(l_{1},v_{1})
,
q_{2}^{X}=(l_{2},v_{2})
,
l_{1},l_{2}\in\mathit{Loc}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
,
v_{1},v_{2}\in[\mathit{Clk}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
,
v_{1}\models\varphi
,
v_{2}=v_{1}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}
, and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}(l_{2})
. From Definition
14
of the conjunction for TIOA it follows that
l_{1}=(l_{1}^{1},l_{1}^{2})
,
l_{2}=(l_{2}^{1},l_{2}^{2})
,
l_{1}^{2}=l_{2}^{2}=l^{2}
, and
\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{2})=\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})\wedge\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
. Since
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{2})
, it follows that
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{2}^{1})
and
v_{2}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l^{2})
.

It now follows directly from Lemma
6
that
q_{1}\xlongrightarrow{a}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
.

∎

Lemma
7
.

Given two TIOAs
A^{i}=(\mathit{Loc}^{i},l_{0}^{i},\mathit{Act}^{i},\mathit{Clk}^{i},E^{i},\mathit{Inv}^{i}),i=1,2
where
\mathit{Act}_{i}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{o}^{2}=\emptyset\wedge\mathit{Act}_{o}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}_{i}^{2}=\emptyset
. Let
Q\subseteq\mathit{Loc}^{1}\times\mathit{Loc}^{2}\times[(\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cup\mathit{Clk}^{2})\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
. Then
\mathrm{err}^{\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(Q)=\mathrm{err}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(Q)
.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
3
that
\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
and
\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
have the same state set. We will show that
\mathrm{err}^{\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(Q)\subseteq\mathrm{err}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(Q)
and
\mathrm{err}^{\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(Q)\subseteq\mathrm{err}^{\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(Q)
. For brevity, we write
X=\llbracket A^{1}\wedge A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
,
Y=\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}\wedge\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}
, and
\mathit{Clk}=\mathit{Clk}^{1}\uplus\mathit{Clk}^{2}
in the rest of this proof. Also, we will use
v^{1}
and
v^{2}
to indicate the part of a valuation
v
of only the clocks of
A^{1}
and
A^{2}
, respectively.

(
\mathrm{err}^{X}(Q)\subseteq\mathrm{err}^{Y}(Q)
)
Consider a state
q^{X}\in\mathrm{err}^{X}(Q)
. From Definition
11
of error states we know that
\exists d\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
s.t.
q^{X}\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!X}
and
\forall d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\forall o!\in\mathit{Act}_{o}\forall q_{2}\in Q^{X}:q^{X}\xlongrightarrow{d^{\prime}}q_{2}\Rightarrow(q_{2}\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!X}\vee\forall q_{3}\in Q^{X}:q_{2}\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!X}q_{3}\Rightarrow q_{3}\in Q)
. From Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA it follows that
q^{X}=(l_{1},v)
for some
l_{1}\in\mathit{Loc}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
and
v\in[\mathit{Clk}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}]
,
v+d\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{1})
, and
v+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{1})\implies[\nexists(l_{1},o!,\varphi,c,l_{3})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}\vee\forall(l_{1},o!,\varphi,c,l_{3})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}:v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi\vee v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{3})\vee(l_{3},v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c})\in Q]
.

From Lemma
4
it follows immediately that
q^{X}\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!X}
implies that
q^{X}\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{d}{}^{\!\!Y}
. So the first condition in the definition of error states holds for
q^{X}
in
Y
.

Now, pick any
d^{\prime}
,
q_{2}
, and
o!
such that
v+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{1})\implies[\nexists(l_{1},o!,\varphi,c,l_{3})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}\vee\forall(l_{1},o!,\varphi,c,l_{3})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}:v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi\vee v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{3})\vee(l_{3},v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c})\in Q]
. The implication holds if
v+d^{\prime}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{1})
or
v+d^{\prime}\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{1})\wedge[\nexists(l_{1},o!,\varphi,c,l_{3})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}\vee\forall(l_{1},o!,\varphi,c,l_{3})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}:v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi\vee v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{3})\vee(l_{3},v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c})\in Q]
. The first case follows directly from Lemma
4
that shows that
q^{X}\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{d^{\prime}}{}^{\!\!Y}
, which ensures that the second condition in the definition of error states holds for
q^{X}
in
Y
. For the second case we again use Lemma
4
, thus
q^{X}\xlongrightarrow{d^{\prime}}{}^{\!\!Y}q_{2}
, where
q_{2}=(l_{1},v+d^{\prime})
. Now consider the two cases in the right-hand side of the implication.

•

\nexists(l_{1},o!,\varphi,c,l_{3})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
. We have to consider the three cases from Definition
14
of the conjunction for TIOA.

–

o!\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}^{2}
. In this case, we know that
\nexists(l_{1}^{1},o!,\varphi^{1},c^{1},l_{3}^{1})\in E^{1}
or
\nexists(l_{1}^{2},o!,\varphi^{2},c^{2},l_{3}^{2})\in E^{2}
(or both). Therefore, it follows from Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA that
(l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime})\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
or
(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime})\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
(or both). Now, from Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!Y}
10
10

10

Alternatively, we could use Lemma
5
to come to the same conclusion. This also holds for the other two cases, where we have to use Corollary
1
instead.
.

–

o!\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{2}
. In this case, we know that
\nexists(l_{1}^{1},o!,\varphi^{1},c^{1},l_{3}^{1})\in E^{1}
. Therefore, it follows from Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA that
(l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime})\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
. Now, from Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!Y}
.

–

o!\in\mathit{Act}^{2}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{1}
. In this case, we know that
\nexists(l_{1}^{2},o!,\varphi^{2},c^{2},l_{3}^{2})\in E^{2}
. Therefore, it follows from Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA that
(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime})\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
. Now, from Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!Y}
.

So, in all three cases we can show that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!Y}
. And note that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))=q_{2}
.

•

\forall(l_{1},o!,\varphi,c,l_{3})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}:v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi\vee v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{3})\vee(l_{3},v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c})\in Q
. For each edge
(l_{1},o!,\varphi,c,l_{3})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
, we have to consider the three cases from Definition
14
of the conjunction for TIOA.

–

o!\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\cap\mathit{Act}^{2}
. In this case, we know that
(l_{1}^{1},o!,\varphi^{1},c^{1},l_{3}^{1})\in E^{1}
,
(l_{1}^{2},o!,\varphi^{2},c^{2},l_{3}^{2})\in E^{2}
,
\varphi=\varphi^{1}\wedge\varphi^{2}
, and
c=c^{1}\cup c^{2}
. Now consider the three cases that should hold for each edge
(l_{1},o!,\varphi,c,l_{3})\in E^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}
.

*

v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi
. In this case, we know that
v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi
implies that
v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi^{1}
or
v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi^{2}
(or both). Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it holds that
v^{1}+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi^{1}
or
v^{2}+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi^{2}
(or both). Therefore, it follows from Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA that
(l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime})\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
or
(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime})\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
(or both). Now, from Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!Y}
.

*

v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{3})
. In this case, we know that
v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{3})
implies that
v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{3}^{1})
or
v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{3}^{2})
(or both). Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it holds that
v^{1}+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{3}^{1})
or
v^{2}+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{2}}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{3}^{2})
(or both). Therefore, it follows from Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA that
(l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime})\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
or
(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime})\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}
(or both). Now, from Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!Y}
.

*

(l_{3},v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c})\in Q
. In this case, assume that
v+d^{\prime}\models\varphi
and
v+d[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{3})
(otherwise, one of the above cases can be used instead). Because
\mathit{Clk}^{1}\cap\mathit{Clk}^{2}=\emptyset
, it follows that
v^{1}+d^{\prime}\models\varphi^{1}
,
v^{2}+d^{\prime}\models\varphi^{2}
,
v^{1}+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}\models\mathit{Inv}^{1}(l_{3}^{1})
, and
v^{2}+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{2}}\models\mathit{Inv}^{2}(l_{3}^{2})
. Therefore, it follows from Definition
3
of the semantic of a TIOA that
(l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime})\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{1}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(l_{3}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}})
and
(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime})\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!\llbracket A^{2}\rrbracket_{\mathrm{sem}}}(l_{3}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{2}})
. Now, from Definition
13
of the conjunction for TIOTS it follows that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!Y}((l_{3}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}),(l_{3}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{2}}))
. And note that
((l_{3}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{1}}),(l_{3}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c^{2}}))=(l_{3}^{1},l_{3}^{2},v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c})=(l_{3},v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c})
.

So, in the first two cases we have shown that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!Y}
11
11

11

Alternatively, we could use Lemma
5
to come to the same conclusion. This also holds for the other two cases, where we have to use Corollary
1
instead.
and in the third case that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!Y}(l_{3},v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c})
.

–

o!\in\mathit{Act}^{1}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{2}
. In this case, we know that
(l_{1}^{1},o!,\varphi^{1},c^{1},l_{3}^{1})\in E^{1}
,

, and

. Now consider the three cases that should hold for each edge

.

v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi
. In this case, we know that
v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi

. Because

. Therefore, it follows from Definition

v+d[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c}\not\models\mathit{Inv}^{A^{1}\wedge A^{2}}(l_{3})
. In this case, we know that

*

(l_{3},v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c})\in Q
. In this case, assume that
v+d^{\prime}\models\varphi
and

(l_{3},v+d^{\prime}[r\mapsto 0]_{r\in c})\in Q

So, in the first two cases we have shown that
((l_{1}^{1},v^{1}+d^{\prime}),(l_{1}^{2},v^{2}+d^{\prime}))\arrownot\xlongrightarrow{o!}{}^{\!\!Y}
and in the third case that

.

–

o!\in\mathit{Act}^{2}\setminus\mathit{Act}^{1}
. In this case, we know that
(l_{1}^{2},o!,\varphi^{2},c^{2},l_{3}^{2})\in E^{2}
,
\varphi=\varphi^{2}
, and

. Now consider the three cases that should hold for each edge

v+d^{\prime}\not\models\varphi

.
